  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.1, F32.2, F32.3, F33.1, F33.2, F33.3\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess what finally made me come in is that I don’t really feel like myself anymore. Not in a dramatic way, exactly, more like… the part of me that used to naturally reach toward things has gone quiet.\n\nI used to be someone who wanted to see people, or at least I’d make the effort. I cared about my work, I got something from it. And outside of work I had things that made me feel connected to myself—yoga, going to galleries, writing in my journal. They weren’t just tasks, they were things I enjoyed, things that helped me feel like a person with an inner life, if that makes sense.\n\nLately I’m mostly just moving through obligations. I do what I have to do, but there isn’t much pull toward anything. It’s not that I’ve decided I don’t like people or don’t value those things anymore. It’s more like the wanting part is muted. And I kept thinking maybe I was just tired, or in a rut, but it’s been concerning enough that I thought I should talk to someone before it becomes… I don’t know, before I disappear further into it.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been about eight months, I think. I can place it pretty clearly because it started around the time we took my older relative into our apartment. At first I thought it was just the adjustment—having someone else there, the routines changing, feeling responsible in a different way—but it didn’t really lift after the first few weeks.\n\nSo… yes, about eight months that I’ve noticed this whole shift in how I’m thinking, how I’m working, and how I’m connecting with people. It’s gradual enough that I kept explaining it away, but when I look back, that’s when it began.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep or appetite, or do you find yourself feeling more down or critical of yourself these days?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can give you a clean answer to that. I haven’t been tracking sleep or appetite in any organized way, so I don’t want to say something too definite and then realize I’m just filling in the blanks.\n\nI mean, my days feel flatter, and I do have moments where I think, “Why can’t I just function the way I used to?” But I don’t know if I’d call it feeling down exactly, or more like frustrated and kind of distant from myself. And the self-critical part… maybe a little, but not in this constant harsh voice way. It’s more that I notice I’m not doing things with the same energy or interest, and then I start picking at that in my head.\n\nAs for appetite, I don’t think there’s been anything obvious. Sleep… I don’t know. Some nights are better than others, like anyone, but I can’t point to a major change that I’ve clearly noticed.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time recently when you experienced pleasure or satisfaction from something you did, even a small thing?\n\nPatient: That’s the thing I’ve been trying to notice, actually. I can do things, but I don’t know that I really get pleasure from them the way I used to.\n\nLike, I’ll go through the motions with work and handle what needs to be handled, and there may be a moment where I think, “Okay, I completed that,” but it doesn’t land as satisfying in the same way. It’s more like checking something off so it stops pressing on me.\n\nAnd with the things that used to give me that feeling—yoga, going to a gallery, even journaling—I either don’t reach for them, or if I do, it feels kind of muted. I can recognize intellectually that they’re good things, things I used to enjoy, but the actual feeling of enjoyment doesn’t really show up much. It’s like the part of me that used to naturally move toward people or activities has gone quiet. I’m mostly just moving through obligations.\n\nDoctor: When you say that enjoyment doesn’t really show up much anymore, how long do you think it’s been since you genuinely felt pleasure in something, even a small thing?\n\nPatient: I think… honestly, it’s hard to pin down one last moment. That’s part of why I came in, because it isn’t like there was one day where I stopped enjoying things. It’s more like that part of me has gotten quieter and quieter.\n\nI used to have these small pulls toward things—wanting to talk to someone after work, wanting to go to yoga because I knew I’d feel better afterward, wanting to stop into a gallery, or sit with my journal and sort things out. And now I mostly don’t reach for any of it. If I do it, it’s more because I think I should, or because it’s on some mental list of “things that are good for me,” not because I actually feel drawn to it.\n\nSo if you’re asking when I last genuinely felt pleasure, I don’t know. I can’t remember a recent clear example where I felt that real satisfaction or warmth from something. What finally brought me in is that I’m mostly just moving through obligations, and the part of me that used to take pleasure in my work and in people and in those small things feels like it’s gone very quiet.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a change in your energy levels or do you feel more fatigued these days, even if you’re not doing anything particularly taxing?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, though I’m a little hesitant because I don’t always experience it as “tired” in the straightforward sense, like I ran a marathon or didn’t sleep. It’s more that I don’t have that inward push I used to have.\n\nWhat finally brought me in is that the part of me that used to naturally reach for people, or take pleasure in things, feels very quiet now. Work, yoga, going to galleries, journaling—those used to give me some sense of energy or direction, even if I was busy or stressed. Now I mostly just move through what I have to do. I can still do obligations, at least some of them, but it feels more like I’m carrying myself through them rather than being engaged.\n\nSo yes, if you mean energy as in motivation or liveliness, that’s definitely changed. It’s not always “I need to lie down” fatigue. It’s more like everything takes more internal effort, even things that shouldn’t be that taxing.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that activities you used to enjoy, like yoga, going to galleries, or journaling, no longer interest you at all, or do you still have some interest in them even if you aren’t doing them right now?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say there’s absolutely no interest, like I’ve decided I hate those things or they mean nothing to me. It’s more… the interest feels faint, or far away.\n\nI can still look at yoga and think, “That would probably be good for me,” or see a gallery listing and know that, in the past, I would have wanted to go. With journaling, I can even remember how it used to help me feel clearer. But that pull to actually do it — the part of me that used to reach for those things naturally — just isn’t really there most of the time.\n\nThat’s really what finally made me think I should talk to someone. It’s not just one hobby dropping off. It feels like the whole part of me that used to reach for people, and take pleasure in my work, yoga, galleries, journaling, all of that, has gone very quiet. I’m mostly moving through obligations now, doing what needs to be done, but not feeling drawn toward much.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling more guilty or blaming yourself for things more than usual, or feeling like you're not living up to who you should be?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it as guilt, exactly. I mean, I do have thoughts like, “I should be doing better,” or “I should be handling things more like myself,” but I don’t know if that’s guilt in the sense of feeling like I’ve done something wrong.\n\nIt’s more… frustration, maybe, or disappointment that I’m not functioning the way I expect myself to. Especially because I know what I would tell someone else in my position — I’d probably be much more compassionate with them than I am with myself. But when it’s me, I can get stuck on this idea that I should be more engaged, more present, more able to just do the things I know matter to me.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’m walking around blaming myself for everything, though. It’s not like I’m thinking I’m a bad person or that everything is my fault. It’s more this low-level sense of not quite measuring up to the version of myself I remember being.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall if there was a time in the past few weeks where you felt no interest or pleasure in activities you used to enjoy, even things that were usually reliable sources of comfort or happiness for you?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I can. Not one single dramatic moment, exactly, but there have been several times in the past few weeks where I noticed it very clearly.\n\nLike, I’ll think about going to yoga, or I’ll see something about a gallery opening, and I know intellectually that those are things I used to enjoy. They weren’t just “activities” for me — they were sort of how I stayed connected to myself. Same with journaling. It used to feel like a way to sort through the noise and feel more grounded. And work, too, even though it’s demanding, used to give me some sense of purpose.\n\nBut lately it’s like the part of me that used to reach for those things has gone quiet. I’m not sitting there thinking, “I hate this” or “there’s no point ever doing anything.” It’s more blank than that. I can recognize that something used to comfort me or make me feel alive, but I don’t feel the pull toward it.\n\nSo I mostly just move through the things I have to do — obligations, appointments, basic routines — but the pleasure or interest part is very muted. That’s really what scared me enough to come in, because it isn’t just that I skipped yoga for a week or didn’t feel like writing one night. It feels broader, like my connection to people and to the things that usually mattered to me has gotten very faint.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times recently when nothing — even things you’ve always enjoyed — felt pleasurable or meaningful, like there was just a total absence of feeling?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it a total absence of feeling every single time, but… yes, there have been moments where it’s felt very close to that. Like the things that used to have some kind of emotional charge for me — not even big happiness, necessarily, just meaning or comfort — they feel sort of flattened out.\n\nWhat finally brought me in is that it feels like the part of me that used to reach for people, or take pleasure in my work, or want to go to yoga, or wander around a gallery, or even just sit down and journal, has gone very quiet. Like I remember the meaning, but I can’t quite feel it.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days recently where absolutely nothing — even things you used to care about — felt meaningful or brought you any sense of pleasure at all?\n\nPatient: Yes… there have been days like that, or close enough that it worries me. I don’t want to overstate it and say absolutely every single thing meant nothing, because I can still sort of understand, intellectually, that things matter. But feeling it is different.\n\nWhat finally made me think I needed help was that the part of me that used to naturally reach for people, or feel some satisfaction from my work, or want to go to yoga, or be curious about a gallery, or even sit down and journal just to get my thoughts out… that part has gone very quiet. It’s like I’m still aware of the outline of who I am, but I’m not really inhabiting it the same way.\n\nSo yes, there are days where I’m mostly just moving through obligations. Yes… I mean, I keep hesitating with the word “nothing,” because it sounds so final, and I can still understand in my head that things matter. But there have been days where it feels like nothing is actually landing emotionally.

That’s really what brought me in. I can do things, but I don’t know that I really get pleasure from them the way I used to.\n\nLike, I’ll go through the motions with work and handle what needs to be handled, and there may be a moment where I think, “Okay, I completed that,” but it doesn’t land as satisfying in the same way. It’s more like checking something off so it stops pressing on me.\n\nAnd with the things that used to give me that feeling—yoga, going to a gallery, even journaling—I either don’t reach for them, or if I do, it feels kind of muted. I can recognize intellectually that they’re good things, things I used to enjoy, but the actual feeling of enjoyment doesn’t really show up much. It’s like the part of me that used to naturally move toward people or activities has gone quiet. I’m mostly just moving through obligations.\n\nDoctor: When you say that enjoyment doesn’t really show up much anymore, how long do you think it’s been since you genuinely felt pleasure in something, even a small thing?\n\nPatient: I think… honestly, it’s hard to pin down one last moment. That’s part of why I came in, because it isn’t like there was one day where I stopped enjoying things. It’s more like that part of me has gotten quieter and quieter.\n\nI used to have these small pulls toward things—wanting to talk to someone after work, wanting to go to yoga because I knew I’d feel better afterward, wanting to stop into a gallery, or sit with my journal and sort things out. And now I mostly don’t reach for any of it. If I do it, it’s more because I think I should, or because it’s on some mental list of “things that are good for me,” not because I actually feel drawn to it.\n\nSo if you’re asking when I last genuinely felt pleasure, I don’t know. I can’t remember a recent clear example where I felt that real satisfaction or warmth from something. What finally brought me in is that I’m mostly just moving through obligations, and the part of me that used to take pleasure in my work and in people and in those small things feels like it’s gone very quiet.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a change in your energy levels or do you feel more fatigued these days, even if you’re not doing anything particularly taxing?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, though I’m a little hesitant because I don’t always experience it as “tired” in the straightforward sense, like I ran a marathon or didn’t sleep. It’s more that I don’t have that inward push I used to have.\n\nWhat finally brought me in is that the part of me that used to naturally reach for people, or take pleasure in things, feels very quiet now. Work, yoga, going to galleries, journaling—those used to give me some sense of energy or direction, even if I was busy or stressed. Now I mostly just move through what I have to do. I can still do obligations, at least some of them, but it feels more like I’m carrying myself through them rather than being engaged.\n\nSo yes, if you mean energy as in motivation or liveliness, that’s definitely changed. It’s not always “I need to lie down” fatigue. It’s more like everything takes more internal effort, even things that shouldn’t be that taxing.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that activities you used to enjoy, like yoga, going to galleries, or journaling, no longer interest you at all, or do you still have some interest in them even if you aren’t doing them right now?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say there’s absolutely no interest, like I’ve decided I hate those things or they mean nothing to me. It’s more… the interest feels faint, or far away.\n\nI can still look at yoga and think, “That would probably be good for me,” or see a gallery listing and know that, in the past, I would have wanted to go. With journaling, I can even remember how it used to help me feel clearer. But that pull to actually do it — the part of me that used to reach for those things naturally — just isn’t really there most of the time.\n\nThat’s really what finally made me think I should talk to someone. It’s not just one hobby dropping off. It feels like the whole part of me that used to reach for people, and take pleasure in my work, yoga, galleries, journaling, all of that, has gone very quiet. I’m mostly moving through obligations now, doing what needs to be done, but not feeling drawn toward much.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling more guilty or blaming yourself for things more than usual, or feeling like you're not living up to who you should be?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it as guilt, exactly. I mean, I do have thoughts like, “I should be doing better,” or “I should be handling things more like myself,” but I don’t know if that’s guilt in the sense of feeling like I’ve done something wrong.\n\nIt’s more… frustration, maybe, or disappointment that I’m not functioning the way I expect myself to. Especially because I know what I would tell someone else in my position — I’d probably be much more compassionate with them than I am with myself. But when it’s me, I can get stuck on this idea that I should be more engaged, more present, more able to just do the things I know matter to me.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’m walking around blaming myself for everything, though. It’s not like I’m thinking I’m a bad person or that everything is my fault. It’s more this low-level sense of not quite measuring up to the version of myself I remember being.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall if there was a time in the past few weeks where you felt no interest or pleasure in activities you used to enjoy, even things that were usually reliable sources of comfort or happiness for you?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I can. Not one single dramatic moment, exactly, but there have been several times in the past few weeks where I noticed it very clearly.\n\nLike, I’ll think about going to yoga, or I’ll see something about a gallery opening, and I know intellectually that those are things I used to enjoy. They weren’t just “activities” for me — they were sort of how I stayed connected to myself. Same with journaling. It used to feel like a way to sort through the noise and feel more grounded. And work, too, even though it’s demanding, used to give me some sense of purpose.\n\nBut lately it’s like the part of me that used to reach for those things has gone quiet. I’m not sitting there thinking, “I hate this” or “there’s no point ever doing anything.” It’s more blank than that. I can recognize that something used to comfort me or make me feel alive, but I don’t feel the pull toward it.\n\nSo I mostly just move through the things I have to do — obligations, appointments, basic routines — but the pleasure or interest part is very muted. That’s really what scared me enough to come in, because it isn’t just that I skipped yoga for a week or didn’t feel like writing one night. It feels broader, like my connection to people and to the things that usually mattered to me has gotten very faint.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times recently when nothing — even things you’ve always enjoyed — felt pleasurable or meaningful, like there was just a total absence of feeling?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it a total absence of feeling every single time, but… yes, there have been moments where it’s felt very close to that. Like the things that used to have some kind of emotional charge for me — not even big happiness, necessarily, just meaning or comfort — they feel sort of flattened out.\n\nWhat finally brought me in is that it feels like the part of me that used to reach for people, or take pleasure in my work, or want to go to yoga, or wander around a gallery, or even just sit down and journal, has gone very quiet. I still know those things matter to me, or mattered to me. I can describe why they mattered. The pleasure or meaning part is very faint, almost like I remember that those things mattered to me, but I can’t really feel it in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where nothing — not even things you used to care about — brought any sense of pleasure or meaning at all?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I keep hesitating with the word “nothing,” because it sounds so final, and I can still understand in my head that things matter. But there have been days where it feels like nothing is actually landing emotionally.\n\nThat’s really what brought me in. The part of me that used to reach toward people, or feel something from my work, or want to go to yoga, or walk around a gallery, or even just journal because it helped me feel connected to myself… it’s gone very quiet. Not gone in a dramatic way, exactly. More like it’s been turned down so low that I can barely access it.\n\nI still do things because I’m supposed to do them. I answer what I need to answer, I show up where I need to show up, I keep moving through obligations. But it doesn’t feel like I’m being pulled by interest or pleasure or meaning. It’s more like I remember that those things used to mean something to me, but in the moment, I can’t really feel it.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you had days where absolutely nothing — not even things you used to enjoy or care about — brought any sense of pleasure or meaning at all?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 49 / mental_health_or_substance_abuse_social_worker / graduate\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Negative_Symptoms\nmanifestation: \"What finally brought me in is that I feel like the part of me that used to reach for people and take pleasure in my work, yoga, galleries, even journaling, has gone very quiet, and I’m mostly just moving through obligations.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… yes, I have. I always get a little caught on the word “absolutely,” because part of me wants to be precise and not sound like I’m exaggerating. I can still know, in my head, that certain things matter. But on some days, emotionally, it feels like almost nothing comes through.

What finally brought me in is that the part of me that used to naturally reach for people has gone very quiet. And not just people — work used to give me some sense of purpose, or at least satisfaction. Yoga used to help me feel grounded. Galleries used to make me feel curious or moved. Journaling used to feel like a way back into myself. Lately, even those things feel very far away from me.

I can still do what needs to be done. I can show up, answer things, go through the motions, keep the basic structure of my life going. But it feels like I’m mostly just moving through obligations, not really getting pleasure or meaning from them. It’s more like I remember that I used to care, rather than actually feeling that care in the moment.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.1, F32.2, F32.3, F33.1, F33.2, F33.3\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess what finally made me come in is that I don’t really feel like myself anymore. Not in a dramatic way, exactly, more like… the part of me that used to naturally reach toward things has gone quiet.\n\nI used to be someone who wanted to see people, or at least I’d make the effort. I cared about my work, I got something from it. And outside of work I had things that made me feel connected to myself—yoga, going to galleries, writing in my journal. They weren’t just tasks, they were things I enjoyed, things that helped me feel like a person with an inner life, if that makes sense.\n\nLately I’m mostly just moving through obligations. I do what I have to do, but there isn’t much pull toward anything. It’s not that I’ve decided I don’t like people or don’t value those things anymore. It’s more like the wanting part is muted. And I kept thinking maybe I was just tired, or in a rut, but it’s been concerning enough that I thought I should talk to someone before it becomes… I don’t know, before I disappear further into it.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been about eight months, I think. I can place it pretty clearly because it started around the time we took my older relative into our apartment. At first I thought it was just the adjustment—having someone else there, the routines changing, feeling responsible in a different way—but it didn’t really lift after the first few weeks.\n\nSo… yes, about eight months that I’ve noticed this whole shift in how I’m thinking, how I’m working, and how I’m connecting with people. It’s gradual enough that I kept explaining it away, but when I look back, that’s when it began.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep or appetite, or do you find yourself feeling more down or critical of yourself these days?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can give you a clean answer to that. I haven’t been tracking sleep or appetite in any organized way, so I don’t want to say something too definite and then realize I’m just filling in the blanks.\n\nI mean, my days feel flatter, and I do have moments where I think, “Why can’t I just function the way I used to?” But I don’t know if I’d call it feeling down exactly, or more like frustrated and kind of distant from myself. And the self-critical part… maybe a little, but not in this constant harsh voice way. It’s more that I notice I’m not doing things with the same energy or interest, and then I start picking at that in my head.\n\nAs for appetite, I don’t think there’s been anything obvious. Sleep… I don’t know. Some nights are better than others, like anyone, but I can’t point to a major change that I’ve clearly noticed.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time recently when you experienced pleasure or satisfaction from something you did, even a small thing?\n\nPatient: That’s the thing I’ve been trying to notice, actually. I can do things, but I don’t know that I really get pleasure from them the way I used to.\n\nLike, I’ll go through the motions with work and handle what needs to be handled, and there may be a moment where I think, “Okay, I completed that,” but it doesn’t land as satisfying in the same way. It’s more like checking something off so it stops pressing on me.\n\nAnd with the things that used to give me that feeling—yoga, going to a gallery, even journaling—I either don’t reach for them, or if I do, it feels kind of muted. I can recognize intellectually that they’re good things, things I used to enjoy, but the actual feeling of enjoyment doesn’t really show up much. It’s like the part of me that used to naturally move toward people or activities has gone quiet. I’m mostly just moving through obligations.\n\nDoctor: When you say that enjoyment doesn’t really show up much anymore, how long do you think it’s been since you genuinely felt pleasure in something, even a small thing?\n\nPatient: I think… honestly, it’s hard to pin down one last moment. That’s part of why I came in, because it isn’t like there was one day where I stopped enjoying things. It’s more like that part of me has gotten quieter and quieter.\n\nI used to have these small pulls toward things—wanting to talk to someone after work, wanting to go to yoga because I knew I’d feel better afterward, wanting to stop into a gallery, or sit with my journal and sort things out. And now I mostly don’t reach for any of it. If I do it, it’s more because I think I should, or because it’s on some mental list of “things that are good for me,” not because I actually feel drawn to it.\n\nSo if you’re asking when I last genuinely felt pleasure, I don’t know. I can’t remember a recent clear example where I felt that real satisfaction or warmth from something. What finally brought me in is that I’m mostly just moving through obligations, and the part of me that used to take pleasure in my work and in people and in those small things feels like it’s gone very quiet.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a change in your energy levels or do you feel more fatigued these days, even if you’re not doing anything particularly taxing?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, though I’m a little hesitant because I don’t always experience it as “tired” in the straightforward sense, like I ran a marathon or didn’t sleep. It’s more that I don’t have that inward push I used to have.\n\nWhat finally brought me in is that the part of me that used to naturally reach for people, or take pleasure in things, feels very quiet now. Work, yoga, going to galleries, journaling—those used to give me some sense of energy or direction, even if I was busy or stressed. Now I mostly just move through what I have to do. I can still do obligations, at least some of them, but it feels more like I’m carrying myself through them rather than being engaged.\n\nSo yes, if you mean energy as in motivation or liveliness, that’s definitely changed. It’s not always “I need to lie down” fatigue. It’s more like everything takes more internal effort, even things that shouldn’t be that taxing.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that activities you used to enjoy, like yoga, going to galleries, or journaling, no longer interest you at all, or do you still have some interest in them even if you aren’t doing them right now?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say there’s absolutely no interest, like I’ve decided I hate those things or they mean nothing to me. It’s more… the interest feels faint, or far away.\n\nI can still look at yoga and think, “That would probably be good for me,” or see a gallery listing and know that, in the past, I would have wanted to go. With journaling, I can even remember how it used to help me feel clearer. But that pull to actually do it — the part of me that used to reach for those things naturally — just isn’t really there most of the time.\n\nThat’s really what finally made me think I should talk to someone. It’s not just one hobby dropping off. It feels like the whole part of me that used to reach for people, and take pleasure in my work, yoga, galleries, journaling, all of that, has gone very quiet. I’m mostly moving through obligations now, doing what needs to be done, but not feeling drawn toward much.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling more guilty or blaming yourself for things more than usual, or feeling like you're not living up to who you should be?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it as guilt, exactly. I mean, I do have thoughts like, “I should be doing better,” or “I should be handling things more like myself,” but I don’t know if that’s guilt in the sense of feeling like I’ve done something wrong.\n\nIt’s more… frustration, maybe, or disappointment that I’m not functioning the way I expect myself to. Especially because I know what I would tell someone else in my position — I’d probably be much more compassionate with them than I am with myself. But when it’s me, I can get stuck on this idea that I should be more engaged, more present, more able to just do the things I know matter to me.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’m walking around blaming myself for everything, though. It’s not like I’m thinking I’m a bad person or that everything is my fault. It’s more this low-level sense of not quite measuring up to the version of myself I remember being.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall if there was a time in the past few weeks where you felt no interest or pleasure in activities you used to enjoy, even things that were usually reliable sources of comfort or happiness for you?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I can. Not one single dramatic moment, exactly, but there have been several times in the past few weeks where I noticed it very clearly.\n\nLike, I’ll think about going to yoga, or I’ll see something about a gallery opening, and I know intellectually that those are things I used to enjoy. They weren’t just “activities” for me — they were sort of how I stayed connected to myself. Same with journaling. It used to feel like a way to sort through the noise and feel more grounded. And work, too, even though it’s demanding, used to give me some sense of purpose.\n\nBut lately it’s like the part of me that used to reach for those things has gone quiet. I’m not sitting there thinking, “I hate this” or “there’s no point ever doing anything.” It’s more blank than that. I can recognize that something used to comfort me or make me feel alive, but I don’t feel the pull toward it.\n\nSo I mostly just move through the things I have to do — obligations, appointments, basic routines — but the pleasure or interest part is very muted. That’s really what scared me enough to come in, because it isn’t just that I skipped yoga for a week or didn’t feel like writing one night. It feels broader, like my connection to people and to the things that usually mattered to me has gotten very faint.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times recently when nothing — even things you’ve always enjoyed — felt pleasurable or meaningful, like there was just a total absence of feeling?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it a total absence of feeling every single time, but… yes, there have been moments where it’s felt very close to that. Like the things that used to have some kind of emotional charge for me — not even big happiness, necessarily, just meaning or comfort — they feel sort of flattened out.\n\nWhat finally brought me in is that it feels like the part of me that used to reach for people, or take pleasure in my work, or want to go to yoga, or wander around a gallery, or even just sit down and journal, has gone very quiet. I still know those things matter to me, or mattered to me. I can describe why they mattered. But I’m mostly just moving through obligations now, getting through what has to be done, and not really feeling drawn toward anything.\n\nIt’s strange because it isn’t dramatic from the outside. I’m not, you know, throwing everything away or saying I’ll never enjoy anything again. It’s more like there’s this absence where the interest used to be. Like I remember the meaning, but I can’t quite feel it.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days recently where absolutely nothing — even things you used to care about — felt meaningful or brought you any sense of pleasure at all?\n\nPatient: Yes… there have been days like that, or close enough that it worries me. I don’t want to overstate it and say absolutely every single thing meant nothing, because I can still sort of understand, intellectually, that things matter. But feeling it is different.\n\nWhat finally made me think I needed help was that the part of me that used to naturally reach for people, or feel some satisfaction from my work, or want to go to yoga, or be curious about a gallery, or even sit down and journal just to get my thoughts out… that part has gone very quiet. It’s like I’m still aware of the outline of who I am, but I’m not really inhabiting it the same way.\n\nSo yes, there are days where I’m mostly just moving through obligations. I do what I’m supposed to do, or what has to be done, but there isn’t much pleasure in it, and not much sense of meaning while I’m doing it. It feels muted, like I’m remembering that I used to care rather than actually caring in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where nothing — not even things you used to care about — brought any sense of pleasure or meaning at all?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I have, though I keep wanting to qualify it because “nothing at all” sounds so absolute. But there have definitely been days where it felt like almost nothing could reach me in the way it used to.\n\nWhat finally brought me in is that I feel like the part of me that used to reach for people has just gone very quiet. Not gone in a dramatic way, not like I’m rejecting everyone or deciding none of it matters, but more like that instinct to connect or to be interested just isn’t there the same way. And it’s the same with work, which used to give me some sense of usefulness, and yoga, and going to galleries, and even journaling. Those were things that usually helped me feel like myself.\n\nNow I can still go through the motions. I can show up where I’m supposed to, answer messages if I have to, get basic things done. But it feels more like moving through obligations than actually wanting to do any of it. The pleasure or meaning part is very faint, almost like I remember that those things mattered to me, but I can’t really feel it in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where nothing — not even things you used to care about — brought any sense of pleasure or meaning at all?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I keep hesitating with the word “nothing,” because it sounds so final, and I can still understand in my head that things matter. But there have been days where it feels like nothing is actually landing emotionally.\n\nThat’s really what brought me in. The part of me that used to reach toward people, or feel something from my work, or want to go to yoga, or walk around a gallery, or even just journal because it helped me feel connected to myself… it’s gone very quiet. Not gone in a dramatic way, exactly. More like it’s been turned down so low that I can barely access it.\n\nI still do things because I’m supposed to do them. I answer what I need to answer, I show up where I need to show up, I keep moving through obligations. But it doesn’t feel like I’m being pulled by interest or pleasure or meaning. It’s more like I remember that those things used to mean something to me, but in the moment, I can’t really feel it.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where absolutely nothing — not even things you used to enjoy or care about — brought any sense of pleasure or meaning at all?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have. I always get a little caught on the word “absolutely,” because part of me wants to be precise and not sound like I’m exaggerating. I can still know, in my head, that certain things matter. But on some days, emotionally, it feels like almost nothing comes through.\n\nWhat finally brought me in is that the part of me that used to naturally reach for people has gone very quiet. And not just people — work used to give me some sense of purpose, or at least satisfaction. Yoga used to help me feel grounded. Galleries used to make me feel curious or moved. Journaling used to feel like a way back into myself. Lately, even those things feel very far away from me.\n\nI can still do what needs to be done. I can show up, answer things, go through the motions, keep the basic structure of my life going. But it feels like I’m mostly just moving through obligations, not really getting pleasure or meaning from them. It’s more like I remember that I used to care, rather than actually feeling that care in the moment.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Not one single dramatic moment, exactly, but there have been several times in the past few weeks where I noticed it very clearly.\n\nLike, I’ll think about going to yoga, or I’ll see something about a gallery opening, and I know intellectually that those are things I used to enjoy. They weren’t just “activities” for me — they were sort of how I stayed connected to myself. Same with journaling. It used to feel like a way to sort through the noise and feel more grounded. And work, too, even though it’s demanding, used to give me some sense of purpose.\n\nBut lately it’s like the part of me that used to reach for those things has gone quiet. I’m not sitting there thinking, “I hate this” or “there’s no point ever doing anything.” It’s more blank than that. I can recognize that something used to comfort me or make me feel alive, but I don’t feel the pull toward it.\n\nSo I mostly just move through the things I have to do — obligations, appointments, basic routines — but the pleasure or interest part is very muted. That’s really what scared me enough to come in, because it isn’t just that I skipped yoga for a week or didn’t feel like writing one night. It feels broader, like my connection to people and to the things that usually mattered to me has gotten very faint.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times recently when nothing — even things you’ve always enjoyed — felt pleasurable or meaningful, like there was just a total absence of feeling?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it a total absence of feeling every single time, but… yes, there have been moments where it’s felt very close to that. Like the things that used to have some kind of emotional charge for me — not even big happiness, necessarily, just meaning or comfort — they feel sort of flattened out.\n\nWhat finally brought me in is that it feels like the part of me that used to reach for people, or take pleasure in my work, or want to go to yoga, or wander around a gallery, or even just sit down and journal, has gone very quiet. I still know those things matter to me, or mattered to me. I can describe why they mattered. But I’m mostly just moving through obligations now, getting through what has to be done, and not really feeling drawn toward anything.\n\nIt’s strange because it isn’t dramatic from the outside. I’m not, you know, throwing everything away or saying I’ll never enjoy anything again. It’s more like there’s this absence where the interest used to be. Like I remember the meaning, but I can’t quite feel it.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days recently where absolutely nothing — even things you used to care about — felt meaningful or brought you any sense of pleasure at all?\n\nPatient: Yes… there have been days like that, or close enough that it worries me. I don’t want to overstate it and say absolutely every single thing meant nothing, because I can still sort of understand, intellectually, that things matter. But feeling it is different.\n\nWhat finally made me think I needed help was that the part of me that used to naturally reach for people, or feel some satisfaction from my work, or want to go to yoga, or be curious about a gallery, or even sit down and journal just to get my thoughts out… that part has gone very quiet. It’s like I’m still aware of the outline of who I am, but I’m not really inhabiting it the same way.\n\nSo yes, there are days where I’m mostly just moving through obligations. I do what I’m supposed to do, or what has to be done, but there isn’t much pleasure in it, and not much sense of meaning while I’m doing it. It feels muted, like I’m remembering that I used to care rather than actually caring in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where nothing — not even things you used to care about — brought any sense of pleasure or meaning at all?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I have, though I keep wanting to qualify it because “nothing at all” sounds so absolute. Yes… I think that’s a fair way to put it. I mean, “no sense of pleasure or meaning” sounds very stark, and I keep wanting to soften it, but yes, there are days where it’s like the things that used to matter most to me just don’t reach me.

With people, especially, I used to have this instinct to reach out or check in or want to be in the room with someone, and now I often don’t feel that pull. And with work, which has always been important to me, I can still do what I’m supposed to do, but it doesn’t give me that same feeling of usefulness or connection.

Yoga, galleries, journaling — those were things I used to count on to make me feel grounded or interested or like myself. Now I might still do them, or think I should do them, but it feels more like I’m completing a task than getting anything from it. Nearly all day, sometimes, I’m just moving through obligations and remembering that these things used to mean something, rather than actually feeling that they do.

Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess what finally made me come in is that I don’t really feel like myself anymore. Not in a dramatic way, exactly, more like… the part of me that used to naturally reach toward things has gone quiet.\n\nI used to be someone who wanted to see people, or at least I’d make the effort. I cared about my work, I got something from it. And outside of work I had things that made me feel connected to myself—yoga, going to galleries, writing in my journal. They weren’t just tasks, they were things I enjoyed, things that helped me feel like a person with an inner life, if that makes sense.\n\nLately I’m mostly just moving through obligations. I do what I have to do, but there isn’t much pull toward anything. It’s not that I’ve decided I don’t like people or don’t value those things anymore. It’s more like the wanting part is muted. And I kept thinking maybe I was just tired, or in a rut, but it’s been concerning enough that I thought I should talk to someone before it becomes… I don’t know, before I disappear further into it.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been about eight months, I think. I can place it pretty clearly because it started around the time we took my older relative into our apartment. At first I thought it was just the adjustment—having someone else there, the routines changing, feeling responsible in a different way—but it didn’t really lift after the first few weeks.\n\nSo… yes, about eight months that I’ve noticed this whole shift in how I’m thinking, how I’m working, and how I’m connecting with people. It’s gradual enough that I kept explaining it away, but when I look back, that’s when it began.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep or appetite, or do you find yourself feeling more down or critical of yourself these days?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can give you a clean answer to that. I haven’t been tracking sleep or appetite in any organized way, so I don’t want to say something too definite and then realize I’m just filling in the blanks.\n\nI mean, my days feel flatter, and I do have moments where I think, “Why can’t I just function the way I used to?” But I don’t know if I’d call it feeling down exactly, or more like frustrated and kind of distant from myself. And the self-critical part… maybe a little, but not in this constant harsh voice way. It’s more that I notice I’m not doing things with the same energy or interest, and then I start picking at that in my head.\n\nAs for appetite, I don’t think there’s been anything obvious. Sleep… I don’t know. Some nights are better than others, like anyone, but I can’t point to a major change that I’ve clearly noticed.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time recently when you experienced pleasure or satisfaction from something you did, even a small thing?\n\nPatient: That’s the thing I’ve been trying to notice, actually. I can do things, but I don’t know that I really get pleasure from them the way I used to.\n\nLike, I’ll go through the motions with work and handle what needs to be handled, and there may be a moment where I think, “Okay, I completed that,” but it doesn’t land as satisfying in the same way. It’s more like checking something off so it stops pressing on me.\n\nAnd with the things that used to give me that feeling—yoga, going to a gallery, even journaling—I either don’t reach for them, or if I do, it feels kind of muted. I can recognize intellectually that they’re good things, things I used to enjoy, but the actual feeling of enjoyment doesn’t really show up much. It’s like the part of me that used to naturally move toward people or activities has gone quiet. I’m mostly just moving through obligations.\n\nDoctor: When you say that enjoyment doesn’t really show up much anymore, how long do you think it’s been since you genuinely felt pleasure in something, even a small thing?\n\nPatient: I think… honestly, it’s hard to pin down one last moment. That’s part of why I came in, because it isn’t like there was one day where I stopped enjoying things. It’s more like that part of me has gotten quieter and quieter.\n\nI used to have these small pulls toward things—wanting to talk to someone after work, wanting to go to yoga because I knew I’d feel better afterward, wanting to stop into a gallery, or sit with my journal and sort things out. And now I mostly don’t reach for any of it. If I do it, it’s more because I think I should, or because it’s on some mental list of “things that are good for me,” not because I actually feel drawn to it.\n\nSo if you’re asking when I last genuinely felt pleasure, I don’t know. I can’t remember a recent clear example where I felt that real satisfaction or warmth from something. What finally brought me in is that I’m mostly just moving through obligations, and the part of me that used to take pleasure in my work and in people and in those small things feels like it’s gone very quiet.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a change in your energy levels or do you feel more fatigued these days, even if you’re not doing anything particularly taxing?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, though I’m a little hesitant because I don’t always experience it as “tired” in the straightforward sense, like I ran a marathon or didn’t sleep. It’s more that I don’t have that inward push I used to have.\n\nWhat finally brought me in is that the part of me that used to naturally reach for people, or take pleasure in things, feels very quiet now. Work, yoga, going to galleries, journaling—those used to give me some sense of energy or direction, even if I was busy or stressed. Now I mostly just move through what I have to do. I can still do obligations, at least some of them, but it feels more like I’m carrying myself through them rather than being engaged.\n\nSo yes, if you mean energy as in motivation or liveliness, that’s definitely changed. It’s not always “I need to lie down” fatigue. It’s more like everything takes more internal effort, even things that shouldn’t be that taxing.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that activities you used to enjoy, like yoga, going to galleries, or journaling, no longer interest you at all, or do you still have some interest in them even if you aren’t doing them right now?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say there’s absolutely no interest, like I’ve decided I hate those things or they mean nothing to me. It’s more… the interest feels faint, or far away.\n\nI can still look at yoga and think, “That would probably be good for me,” or see a gallery listing and know that, in the past, I would have wanted to go. With journaling, I can even remember how it used to help me feel clearer. But that pull to actually do it — the part of me that used to reach for those things naturally — just isn’t really there most of the time.\n\nThat’s really what finally made me think I should talk to someone. It’s not just one hobby dropping off. It feels like the whole part of me that used to reach for people, and take pleasure in my work, yoga, galleries, journaling, all of that, has gone very quiet. I’m mostly moving through obligations now, doing what needs to be done, but not feeling drawn toward much.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling more guilty or blaming yourself for things more than usual, or feeling like you're not living up to who you should be?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it as guilt, exactly. I mean, I do have thoughts like, “I should be doing better,” or “I should be handling things more like myself,” but I don’t know if that’s guilt in the sense of feeling like I’ve done something wrong.\n\nIt’s more… frustration, maybe, or disappointment that I’m not functioning the way I expect myself to. Especially because I know what I would tell someone else in my position — I’d probably be much more compassionate with them than I am with myself. But when it’s me, I can get stuck on this idea that I should be more engaged, more present, more able to just do the things I know matter to me.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’m walking around blaming myself for everything, though. It’s not like I’m thinking I’m a bad person or that everything is my fault. It’s more this low-level sense of not quite measuring up to the version of myself I remember being.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall if there was a time in the past few weeks where you felt no interest or pleasure in activities you used to enjoy, even things that were usually reliable sources of comfort or happiness for you?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I can. Not one single dramatic moment, exactly, but there have been several times in the past few weeks where I noticed it very clearly.\n\nLike, I’ll think about going to yoga, or I’ll see something about a gallery opening, and I know intellectually that those are things I used to enjoy. They weren’t just “activities” for me — they were sort of how I stayed connected to myself. Same with journaling. It used to feel like a way to sort through the noise and feel more grounded. And work, too, even though it’s demanding, used to give me some sense of purpose.\n\nBut lately it’s like the part of me that used to reach for those things has gone quiet. I’m not sitting there thinking, “I hate this” or “there’s no point ever doing anything.” It’s more blank than that. I can recognize that something used to comfort me or make me feel alive, but I don’t feel the pull toward it.\n\nSo I mostly just move through the things I have to do — obligations, appointments, basic routines — but the pleasure or interest part is very muted. That’s really what scared me enough to come in, because it isn’t just that I skipped yoga for a week or didn’t feel like writing one night. It feels broader, like my connection to people and to the things that usually mattered to me has gotten very faint.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times recently when nothing — even things you’ve always enjoyed — felt pleasurable or meaningful, like there was just a total absence of feeling?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it a total absence of feeling every single time, but… yes, there have been moments where it’s felt very close to that. Like the things that used to have some kind of emotional charge for me — not even big happiness, necessarily, just meaning or comfort — they feel sort of flattened out.\n\nWhat finally brought me in is that it feels like the part of me that used to reach for people, or take pleasure in my work, or want to go to yoga, or wander around a gallery, or even just sit down and journal, has gone very quiet. I still know those things matter to me, or mattered to me. I can describe why they mattered. But I’m mostly just moving through obligations now, getting through what has to be done, and not really feeling drawn toward anything.\n\nIt’s strange because it isn’t dramatic from the outside. I’m not, you know, throwing everything away or saying I’ll never enjoy anything again. It’s more like there’s this absence where the interest used to be. Like I remember the meaning, but I can’t quite feel it.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days recently where absolutely nothing — even things you used to care about — felt meaningful or brought you any sense of pleasure at all?\n\nPatient: Yes… there have been days like that, or close enough that it worries me. I don’t want to overstate it and say absolutely every single thing meant nothing, because I can still sort of understand, intellectually, that things matter. But feeling it is different.\n\nWhat finally made me think I needed help was that the part of me that used to naturally reach for people, or feel some satisfaction from my work, or want to go to yoga, or be curious about a gallery, or even sit down and journal just to get my thoughts out… that part has gone very quiet. It’s like I’m still aware of the outline of who I am, but I’m not really inhabiting it the same way.\n\nSo yes, there are days where I’m mostly just moving through obligations. I do what I’m supposed to do, or what has to be done, but there isn’t much pleasure in it, and not much sense of meaning while I’m doing it. It feels muted, like I’m remembering that I used to care rather than actually caring in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where nothing — not even things you used to care about — brought any sense of pleasure or meaning at all?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I have, though I keep wanting to qualify it because “nothing at all” sounds so absolute. But there have definitely been days where it felt like almost nothing could reach me in the way it used to.\n\nWhat finally brought me in is that I feel like the part of me that used to reach for people has just gone very quiet. Not gone in a dramatic way, not like I’m rejecting everyone or deciding none of it matters, but more like that instinct to connect or to be interested just isn’t there the same way. And it’s the same with work, which used to give me some sense of usefulness, and yoga, and going to galleries, and even journaling. Those were things that usually helped me feel like myself.\n\nNow I can still go through the motions. I can show up where I’m supposed to, answer messages if I have to, get basic things done. But it feels more like moving through obligations than actually wanting to do any of it. The pleasure or meaning part is very faint, almost like I remember that those things mattered to me, but I can’t really feel it in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where nothing — not even things you used to care about — brought any sense of pleasure or meaning at all?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I keep hesitating with the word “nothing,” because it sounds so final, and I can still understand in my head that things matter. But there have been days where it feels like nothing is actually landing emotionally.\n\nThat’s really what brought me in. The part of me that used to reach toward people, or feel something from my work, or want to go to yoga, or walk around a gallery, or even just journal because it helped me feel connected to myself… it’s gone very quiet. Not gone in a dramatic way, exactly. More like it’s been turned down so low that I can barely access it.\n\nI still do things because I’m supposed to do them. I answer what I need to answer, I show up where I need to show up, I keep moving through obligations. But it doesn’t feel like I’m being pulled by interest or pleasure or meaning. It’s more like I remember that those things used to mean something to me, but in the moment, I can’t really feel it.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where absolutely nothing — not even things you used to enjoy or care about — brought any sense of pleasure or meaning at all?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have. I always get a little caught on the word “absolutely,” because part of me wants to be precise and not sound like I’m exaggerating. I can still know, in my head, that certain things matter. But on some days, emotionally, it feels like almost nothing comes through.\n\nWhat finally brought me in is that the part of me that used to naturally reach for people has gone very quiet. And not just people — work used to give me some sense of purpose, or at least satisfaction. Yoga used to help me feel grounded. Galleries used to make me feel curious or moved. Journaling used to feel like a way back into myself. Lately, even those things feel very far away from me.\n\nI can still do what needs to be done. I can show up, answer things, go through the motions, keep the basic structure of my life going. But it feels like I’m mostly just moving through obligations, not really getting pleasure or meaning from them. It’s more like I remember that I used to care, rather than actually feeling that care in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced days where even the things you used to care about most brought no sense of pleasure or meaning, nearly all day long?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s a fair way to put it. I mean, “no sense of pleasure or meaning” sounds very stark, and I keep wanting to soften it, but yes, there are days where it’s like the things that used to matter most to me just don’t reach me.\n\nIt’s not only that I’m tired of them or bored for a while. It feels more like the part of me that used to naturally lean toward things has gone quiet. With people, especially, I used to have this instinct to reach out or check in or want to be in the room with someone, and now I often don’t feel that pull. And with work, which has always been important to me, I can still do what I’m supposed to do, but it doesn’t give me that same feeling of usefulness or connection.\n\nYoga, galleries, journaling — those were things I used to count on to make me feel grounded or interested or like myself. Now I might still do them, or think I should do them, but it feels more like I’m completing a task than getting anything from it. Nearly all day, sometimes, I’m just moving through obligations and remembering that these things used to mean something, rather than actually feeling that they do.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I’m not completely sure how to answer that, honestly. I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed being persistently sad in that classic way, like crying all day or feeling openly miserable from morning to night. It’s more confusing than that for me.

I don’t know that I would describe it as sadness exactly. Sometimes I only realize something is off because the day has this flatness to it, but I’m not sure if that counts as feeling down.

So… I guess my answer is that I haven’t clearly noticed persistent sadness nearly every day. Or at least I don’t feel confident naming it that way.

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… though I hesitate a little at “completely,” because I can still remember that I used to enjoy things, and I can still sort of recognize, intellectually, that they matter to me. But in the actual moment, yes, it can feel like the pleasure part just isn’t there.

That’s really what made me feel like I needed to come in. The part of me that used to naturally reach for people, or feel some satisfaction from work, has gone very quiet. I’m still doing things — I go through the motions, I meet obligations, I can make myself do yoga or think about going to a gallery, I can sit with my journal — but it doesn’t feel the same. It’s like I’m performing the shape of a life I used to feel connected to.

So in the past couple of weeks, yes, there have been days where things I used to enjoy just don’t seem to give me anything. Not comfort, not much pleasure, not that sense of being grounded. I mostly feel like I’m moving through responsibilities and remembering that these things used to mean something.

I can place it pretty clearly because it started around the time we took my older relative into our apartment. At first I thought it was just the adjustment—having someone else there, the routines changing, feeling responsible in a different way—but it didn’t really lift after the first few weeks.\n\nSo… yes, about eight months that I’ve noticed this whole shift in how I’m thinking, how I’m working, and how I’m connecting with people. It’s gradual enough that I kept explaining it away, but when I look back, that’s when it began.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep or appetite, or do you find yourself feeling more down or critical of yourself these days?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can give you a clean answer to that. I can’t remember a recent clear example where I felt that real satisfaction or warmth from something. What finally brought me in is that I’m mostly just moving through obligations, and the part of me that used to take pleasure in my work and in people and in those small things feels like it’s gone very quiet.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a change in your energy levels or do you feel more fatigued these days, even if you’re not doing anything particularly taxing?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, though I’m a little hesitant because I don’t always experience it as “tired” in the straightforward sense, like I ran a marathon or didn’t sleep. It’s more that I don’t have that inward push I used to have.\n\nWhat finally brought me in is that the part of me that used to naturally reach for people, or take pleasure in things, feels very quiet now. Work, yoga, going to galleries, journaling—those used to give me some sense of energy or direction, even if I was busy or stressed. Now I mostly just move through what I have to do. I can still do obligations, at least some of them, but it feels more like I’m carrying myself through them rather than being engaged.\n\nSo yes, if you mean energy as in motivation or liveliness, that’s definitely changed. It’s not always “I need to lie down” fatigue. It’s more like everything takes more internal effort, even things that shouldn’t be that taxing.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that activities you used to enjoy, like yoga, going to galleries, or journaling, no longer interest you at all, or do you still have some interest in them even if you aren’t doing them right now?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say there’s absolutely no interest, like I’ve decided I hate those things or they mean nothing to me. It’s more… the interest feels faint, or far away.\n\nI can still look at yoga and think, “That would probably be good for me,” or see a gallery listing and know that, in the past, I would have wanted to go. With journaling, I can even remember how it used to help me feel clearer. But that pull to actually do it — the part of me that used to reach for those things naturally — just isn’t really there most of the time.\n\nThat’s really what finally made me think I should talk to someone. It’s not just one hobby dropping off. It feels like the whole part of me that used to reach for people, and take pleasure in my work, yoga, galleries, journaling, all of that, has gone very quiet. I’m mostly moving through obligations now, doing what needs to be done, but not feeling drawn toward much.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling more guilty or blaming yourself for things more than usual, or feeling like you're not living up to who you should be?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it as guilt, exactly. I mean, I do have thoughts like, “I should be doing better,” or “I should be handling things more like myself,” but I don’t know if that’s guilt in the sense of feeling like I’ve done something wrong.\n\nIt’s more… frustration, maybe, or disappointment that I’m not functioning the way I expect myself to. Especially because I know what I would tell someone else in my position — I’d probably be much more compassionate with them than I am with myself. But when it’s me, I can get stuck on this idea that I should be more engaged, more present, more able to just do the things I know matter to me.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’m walking around blaming myself for everything, though. It’s not like I’m thinking I’m a bad person or that everything is my fault. It’s more this low-level sense of not quite measuring up to the version of myself I remember being.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall if there was a time in the past few weeks where you felt no interest or pleasure in activities you used to enjoy, even things that were usually reliable sources of comfort or happiness for you?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I can. Not one single dramatic moment, exactly, but there have been several times in the past few weeks where I noticed it very clearly.\n\nLike, I’ll think about going to yoga, or I’ll see something about a gallery opening, and I know intellectually that those are things I used to enjoy. They weren’t just “activities” for me — they were sort of how I stayed connected to myself. Same with journaling. It used to feel like a way to sort through the noise and feel more grounded. And work, too, even though it’s demanding, used to give me some sense of purpose.\n\nBut lately it’s like the part of me that used to reach for those things has gone quiet. I’m not sitting there thinking, “I hate this” or “there’s no point ever doing anything.” It’s more blank than that. I can recognize that something used to comfort me or make me feel alive, but I don’t feel the pull toward it.\n\nSo I mostly just move through the things I have to do — obligations, appointments, basic routines — but the pleasure or interest part is very muted. It feels broader, like my connection to people and to the things that usually mattered to me has gotten very faint.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times recently when nothing — even things you’ve always enjoyed — felt pleasurable or meaningful, like there was just a total absence of feeling?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it a total absence of feeling every single time, but… yes, there have been moments where it’s felt very close to that. Like the things that used to have some kind of emotional charge for me — not even big happiness, necessarily, just meaning or comfort — they feel sort of flattened out.\n\nWhat finally brought me in is that it feels like the part of me that used to reach for people, or take pleasure in my work, or want to go to yoga, or wander around a gallery, or even just sit down and journal, has gone very quiet. I still know those things matter to me, or mattered to me. I can describe why they mattered. Like I remember the meaning, but I can’t quite feel it.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days recently where absolutely nothing — even things you used to care about — felt meaningful or brought you any sense of pleasure at all?\n\nPatient: Yes… there have been days like that, or close enough that it worries me. I don’t want to overstate it and say absolutely every single thing meant nothing, because I can still sort of understand, intellectually, that things matter. But feeling it is different.\n\nWhat finally made me think I needed help was that the part of me that used to naturally reach for people, or feel some satisfaction from my work, or want to go to yoga, or be curious about a gallery, or even sit down and journal just to get my thoughts out… that part has gone very quiet. It’s like I’m still aware of the outline of who I am, but I’m not really inhabiting it the same way.\n\nSo yes, there are days where I’m mostly just moving through obligations. I do what I’m supposed to do, or what has to be done, but there isn’t much pleasure in it, and not much sense of meaning while I’m doing it. It feels muted, like I’m remembering that I used to care rather than actually caring in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where nothing — not even things you used to care about — brought any sense of pleasure or meaning at all?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I have, though I keep wanting to qualify it because “nothing at all” sounds so absolute. But there have definitely been days where it felt like almost nothing could reach me in the way it used to.\n\nWhat finally brought me in is that I feel like the part of me that used to reach for people has just gone very quiet. Not gone in a dramatic way, not like I’m rejecting everyone or deciding none of it matters, but more like that instinct to connect or to be interested just isn’t there the same way. And it’s the same with work, which used to give me some sense of usefulness, and yoga, and going to galleries, and even journaling. Those were things that usually helped me feel like myself.\n\nNow I can still go through the motions. I can show up where I’m supposed to, answer messages if I have to, get basic things done. But it feels more like moving through obligations than actually wanting to do any of it. The pleasure or meaning part is very faint, almost like I remember that those things mattered to me, but I can’t really feel it in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where nothing — not even things you used to care about — brought any sense of pleasure or meaning at all?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I keep hesitating with the word “nothing,” because it sounds so final, and I can still understand in my head that things matter. But there have been days where it feels like nothing is actually landing emotionally.\n\nThat’s really what brought me in. The part of me that used to reach toward people, or feel something from my work, or want to go to yoga, or walk around a gallery, or even just journal because it helped me feel connected to myself… it’s gone very quiet. Not gone in a dramatic way, exactly. More like it’s been turned down so low that I can barely access it.\n\nI still do things because I’m supposed to do them. I answer what I need to answer, I show up where I need to show up, I keep moving through obligations. But it doesn’t feel like I’m being pulled by interest or pleasure or meaning. It’s more like I remember that those things used to mean something to me, but in the moment, I can’t really feel it.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where absolutely nothing — not even things you used to enjoy or care about — brought any sense of pleasure or meaning at all?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have. I always get a little caught on the word “absolutely,” because part of me wants to be precise and not sound like I’m exaggerating. I can still know, in my head, that certain things matter. But on some days, emotionally, it feels like almost nothing comes through.\n\nWhat finally brought me in is that the part of me that used to naturally reach for people has gone very quiet. And not just people — work used to give me some sense of purpose, or at least satisfaction. Yoga used to help me feel grounded. Galleries used to make me feel curious or moved. Journaling used to feel like a way back into myself. Lately, even those things feel very far away from me.\n\nI can still do what needs to be done. I can show up, answer things, go through the motions, keep the basic structure of my life going. But it feels like I’m mostly just moving through obligations, not really getting pleasure or meaning from them. It’s more like I remember that I used to care, rather than actually feeling that care in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced days where even the things you used to care about most brought no sense of pleasure or meaning, nearly all day long?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s a fair way to put it. I mean, “no sense of pleasure or meaning” sounds very stark, and I keep wanting to soften it, but yes, there are days where it’s like the things that used to matter most to me just don’t reach me.\n\nIt’s not only that I’m tired of them or bored for a while. It feels more like the part of me that used to naturally lean toward things has gone quiet. With people, especially, I used to have this instinct to reach out or check in or want to be in the room with someone, and now I often don’t feel that pull. And with work, which has always been important to me, I can still do what I’m supposed to do, but it doesn’t give me that same feeling of usefulness or connection.\n\nYoga, galleries, journaling — those were things I used to count on to make me feel grounded or interested or like myself. Now I might still do them, or think I should do them, but it feels more like I’m completing a task than getting anything from it. Nearly all day, sometimes, I’m just moving through obligations and remembering that these things used to mean something, rather than actually feeling that they do.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt persistently down or sad for most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that, honestly. I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed being persistently sad in that classic way, like crying all day or feeling openly miserable from morning to night.
